1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-46.02147pt\raise-65.90228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.69394pt\raise-65.90228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.69394pt\raise-65.90228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 172.06961pt\raise-65.90228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 172.06961pt\raise-65.90228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)\times\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)\text{.}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Finally, we note that a pair
((E,\omega),(\hat{E},\hat{\omega}))
of isomorphism classes of bundles with connection has a well-defined pair
(F,\hat{F})\in\Omega^{2}(X)\times\Omega^{2}(X)
of curvatures. We consider the subgroup

\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}:=\{\mathcal{I}_{y\hat{F}+\hat{y}F}\;|\;y,\hat{y}\in\mathbb{R}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Grb}^{\nabla}(X)\text{.}

This is a non-trivial subgroup, as
\mathcal{I}_{B}\cong\mathcal{I}_{C}
holds if and only if
C-B
is a closed 2-form with integral periods. Now,
F
and
\hat{F}
are closed 2-forms with integral periods, but allowing real multiplies spoils integrality.

Proposition 6.2.6
.

The action of
eq.

6.2.12
,

\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\to\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\text{,}

ha s the following properties:

1.

It acts transitively in the fibres of the projection
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\to\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)\times\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)
.

2.

The stabilizer of each element in the fibre over
(\xi,\hat{\xi})\in\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)\times\mathrm{Bun}^{\nabla}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(X)
with curvature pair
(F,\hat{F})
is the subgroup
\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
.

In particular, the quotient
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)/\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
acts freely and transitively on the fibre over
(\xi,\hat{\xi})
.

We first show that
\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
stabilizes. For this, we have to provide an equivalence

(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i},\varphi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})\sim(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i}+y\hat{F}+\hat{y}F,\varphi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})

of adjusted differential cocycles.
We set
p_{i}:=y
and
\hat{p}_{i}:=-\hat{y}
, as well as
z_{ij}=\hat{z}_{ij}=0
. Moreover, we put
\phi_{i}:=-\hat{y}A_{i}
, and
\tilde{e}_{ij}:=-\hat{y}a_{ij}
. Now,
items

3
and
4
hold since
p_{i}
and
\hat{p}_{i}
are constant.
eqs.

6.2.2
and
6.2.4
follow directly from the definitions. Finally,
eq.

5.6.3
becomes

\tilde{e}_{ij}+\tilde{e}_{jk}=\tilde{e}_{ik}+\hat{y}m_{ijk}

and thus follows from
item

1
.

Next we show that no other group elements stabilize. For this, we suppose that a Deligne 2-cocycle
(B_{i},A_{i},c_{ijk})
acts trivially, i.e., that we have an equivalence between adjusted differential
\mathbb{TD}
-cocycles

(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i},\varphi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})\sim(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i}+B_{i},\varphi_{ij}+A_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk}+c_{ijk})\text{.}

Let
(\phi_{i},p_{i},\hat{p}_{i},z_{ij},\hat{z}_{ij},\tilde{e}_{ij})
be a tuple expressing this equivalence.
We start by looking at
items

3
and
4
, which here result in
\mathrm{d}p_{i}=\mathrm{d}\hat{p}_{i}=0
; in other words, these functions are constant. We further have
z_{ij}=p_{i}-p_{j}
. This means that
[z_{ij}]\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}^{n})
goes to zero under the map to
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{R}^{n})
. But this map is injective, as the relevant part of the long exact sequence is

\ldots\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}^{n})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\ldots

and the second arrow is surjective. Thus, there exist
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
z_{ij}=z_{i}-z_{j}
. Observe that
p_{i}-z_{i}=p_{j}-z_{j}
, i.e., there is a real number
y\in\mathbb{R}
such that
y=p_{i}-z_{i}
. Analogously, we treat
\hat{z}_{ij}
, getting
\hat{y}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\hat{y}=\hat{p}_{i}-\hat{z}_{i}
.
We consider now

f_{ij}(x):=\tilde{e}_{ij}(x)+\hat{y}a_{ij}+\hat{a}_{ij}z_{i}\text{;}

then, one can show using
eq.

5.6.3
that
f_{ij}
trivializes
c_{ijk}
, i.e.,

f_{ik}-f_{ij}-f_{jk}=c_{ijk}\text{.}

Next we define
H_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{i})
by
H_{i}:=-\phi_{i}+z_{i}\hat{A}_{i}+\hat{y}A_{i}
.
Then we compute, using
eq.

6.2.2
and the fact that
\hat{p}_{j}
is constant

A_{ij}=H_{i}-H_{j}-f_{ij}^{*}\theta\text{.}

Finally, we get from
eq.

6.2.4
that

B_{i}=\mathrm{d}H_{i}+y\hat{F}-\hat{y}F\text{.}

Summarizing, the last three equations show that there exist
y,\hat{y}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk})\sim(y\hat{F}-\hat{y}F,0,0)
, i.e.,
(B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk})\in\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
.

It remains to prove the transitivity statement. For this, we suppose that we have two differential cocycles

\displaystyle(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i},\phi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})

\displaystyle(A^{\prime}_{i},\hat{A}^{\prime}_{i},R^{\prime}_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{ij},a^{\prime}_{ij},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{ij},m^{\prime}_{ijk},\hat{m}^{\prime}_{ijk},t^{\prime}_{ijk})

and given equivalences
(p_{i},z_{ij})
and
(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{z}_{ij})
between the cocycles of the projected principal
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-bundles with connection,
(A_{i},a_{ij},m_{ijk})
and
(A^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{ij},m_{ijk}^{\prime})
, and
(\hat{A}_{i},\hat{a}_{ij},\hat{m}_{ijk})
and
(\hat{A}^{\prime}_{i},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{ij},\hat{m}_{ijk}^{\prime})
, respectively. We have to find a Deligne 2-cocycle
(B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk})
such that

(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i}+B_{i},\varphi_{ij}+A_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk}+c_{ijk})\\
\sim(A^{\prime}_{i},\hat{A}^{\prime}_{i},R^{\prime}_{i},\varphi^{\prime}_{ij},a^{\prime}_{ij},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{ij},m^{\prime}_{ijk},\hat{m}^{\prime}_{ijk},t^{\prime}_{ijk})\text{.}

(6.2.13)

This is achieved by the definitions

\displaystyle B_{i}

\displaystyle:=R_{i}^{\prime}-R_{i}-p_{i}\hat{F}

\displaystyle A_{ij}

\displaystyle:=-\varphi_{ij}+\varphi^{\prime}_{ij}-z_{ij}\hat{A}_{j}

\displaystyle c_{ijk}

\displaystyle:=t^{\prime}_{ijk}-t_{ijk}+\hat{p}_{k}m_{ijk}^{\prime}-z_{ij}\hat{a}_{jk}

It is indeed straightforward to check using
items

1
,
6.2.1
,
5.6.2
and
2
that
(B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk})
is a Deligne 2-cocycle.
In order to establish the equivalence
eq.

6.2.13
, we set
\phi_{i}:=0
.
eq.

6.2.4
is then obviously satisfied.
The next part of the equivalence is
eq.

6.2.2
, which here reads

\displaystyle z_{ij}\hat{A}_{j}-z_{ij}\hat{A}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{e}_{ij}^{*}\theta\text{.}

This is satisfied by putting
\tilde{e}_{ij}:=-z_{ij}\hat{p}_{j}
. The last equivalence conditions is now
eq.

5.6.3
, which follows immediately from
items

2
and
1
.
∎

6.3
Kahle-Valentino’s T-duality pairs

In this section we discuss the relation between
differential T-duality correspondences
as introduced in
definition

6.1.2
and
differential T-duality
pairs considered by Kahle-Valentino
[
KV14
]
.

The setting of Kahle-Valentino
[
KV14
]
is different as it does not explicitly involve string backgrounds. Their discussion is also limited to the case of torus dimension
n=1
. At the basis of their formalism is a groupoid version of differential cohomology, of which below we recall a slightly simplified version. We consider
differential cohomology groupoids

\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)
, so that the set of isomorphism classes of objects of
\mathcal{H}^{n}(X)
is the ordinary differential cohomology group
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(X)
. Differential cohomology groupoids are supposed to be equipped with
cup product functors

\cup:\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)\times\mathcal{H}^{q}(X)\to\mathcal{H}^{p+q}(X)\text{.}

Moreover,
they come equipped with a functor

\mathcal{I}:\Omega^{p-1}(X)_{dis}\to\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)\text{,}

where the left hand side denotes groupoid whose objects are all
(p-1)
-forms on
X
, and which has only identity morphisms.
A
geometric trivialization
of an object
\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)
is a differential form
K\in\Omega^{p-1}(X)
and an isomorphism
\tau:\xi\to\mathcal{I}_{K}
in
\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)
.
The set
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{p-1}(X)
acts on the set of all geometric trivializations of
\xi
, where
[\eta]\in\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)
sends
\tau
to
\tau+\eta
, and
K
gets shifted by the “curvature” of
\eta
. This action is free and transitive.

A concrete realization of these groupoids can be obtained using Deligne cocycles w.r.t. a fixed open cover with all finite non-empty intersections contractible, see
[
KV14
, §A.2]
. The objects of
\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)
are Deligne
(p-1)
-cocycles
\xi
, and the morphisms
\xi_{1}\to\xi_{2}
are equivalence classes
[\eta]
of
(p-2)
-cochains
\eta
satisfying
\xi_{2}=\xi_{1}+\mathrm{D}\eta
, where
\mathrm{D}
denotes the Deligne differential, and
\eta_{1}\sim\eta_{2}
if there exists a
(p-3)
-cochain
\beta
with
\eta_{2}=\eta_{1}+\mathrm{D}\beta
. Composition of morphisms is just addition. The cup product on the level of objects is the usual cup product in Deligne cohomology, as recalled below. The functor
\mathcal{I}
is the usual inclusion
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi,0,.,,,0)
of a globally defined differential form as a “topologically trivial” Deligne cocycle. For
p=2
, the groupoid
\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
is equivalent to the groupoid of principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundles with connections, and connection-preserving bundle isomorphisms. Under this equivalence, a geometric trivialization is a (not necessarily flat) section. The free and transitive action by
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X)=C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{T})
is the action of smooth
\mathbb{T}
-valued functions on sections.

Definition 6.3.1
.

A
differential T-duality pair
consists of two objects
\xi,\hat{\xi}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
and a geometric trivialization
\tau:\xi\cup\hat{\xi}\to\mathcal{I}_{K}
.

Kahle-Valentino claim in
[
KV14
, §2.5]
that differential T-duality pairs induce topological T-duality correspondences. We want to sharpen this relation and show that differential T-duality pairs are the same as our differential T-duality correspondences. Their relation to topological T-duality correspondences is then a consequence thereof. In order to proceed, it is necessary to consider an equivalence relation on the set of all differential T-duality pairs over
X
. Unfortunately, Kahle-Valentino do not introduce such relation. Apparently, the most natural definition is the following.

Definition 6.3.2
.

Two differential T-duality pairs
(\xi,\hat{\xi},K,\tau)
and
(\xi^{\prime},\hat{\xi}^{\prime},K^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
over
X
are
equivalent
if
K^{\prime}=K
and there exist isomorphisms
p:\xi\to\xi^{\prime}
and
\hat{p}:\hat{\xi}\to\hat{\xi}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
such that the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.40971pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.40971pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\xi\cup\hat{\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.38397pt\raise 7.29167pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.93057pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p\cup\hat{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.73058pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.28525pt\raise-20.34473pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.40971pt\raise-25.59467pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 42.07014pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 77.73058pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\xi^{\prime}\cup\hat{\xi}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.45558pt\raise-21.73558pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.8978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.73059pt\raise-25.80609pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 36.40971pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{K}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 88.83476pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

in
\mathcal{H}^{4}(X)
is commutative.
The set of equivalence classes of differential T-duality pairs is denoted by
\mathrm{TDP}(X)
.

Note that the projection to the objects
\xi,\hat{\xi}
gives a well-defined map

\mathrm{TDP}(X)\to\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\text{.}

Below, we will prove the following result.

Proposition 6.3.3
.

There is a canonical bijection between equivalence classes of differential T-duality correspondences and equivalence classes of differential T-duality pairs,

\mathrm{T}\text{-}\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{diff}}(X)\cong\mathrm{TDP}(X)\text{,}

such that the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 32.92087pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.92087pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\text{-}\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{diff}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.6987pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.0552pt\raise-30.84149pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 64.80978pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 102.6987pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TDP}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.25058pt\raise-30.84149pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-39.42598pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.92087pt\raise-39.42598pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

is commutative.

Because of the cup product, it is necessary to work with
extended
Deligne cohomology, i.e., in degree
p
with the sheaf complex

\mathbb{Z}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}\to\underline{\Omega}^{1}\to\underline{\Omega}^{2}\to...\to\underline{\Omega}^{p}\text{,}

(6.3.1)

whereas before we worked with the quasi-isomorphic complex
\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(2.0,0.0){\text{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 4.94601pt}}}\end{picture}\mathbb{T}\hskip-0.42677pt\to\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(2.0,0.0){\text{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 4.94601pt}}}\end{picture}\Omega\hskip-0.42677pt^{1}\to...\to\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(2.0,0.0){\text{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 4.94601pt}}}\end{picture}\Omega\hskip-0.42677pt^{p}
. In order to be more precise, let us denote the complex
eq.

6.3.1
by
\mathcal{D}^{q}(p)
, so that, for instance,
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(p)=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{D}^{0}(p)=\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(2.0,0.0){\text{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 5.0849pt}}}\end{picture}\mathbb{R}\hskip-0.42677pt
. The Deligne coboundary operator on the corresponding Čech double complex
C^{r}(\mathcal{D}^{q}(p))
is defined to be
\mathrm{D}^{r,q}:=(-1)^{q+1}\delta^{r}+\mathrm{d}^{q}
, where
\mathrm{d}^{-1}
is the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(2.0,0.0){\text{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 5.0849pt}}}\end{picture}\mathbb{R}\hskip-0.42677pt
.
The cup product of extended Deligne cocycles

\xi=(A^{p-1}_{i},A^{p-2}_{i_{1}i_{2}},...,A^{0}_{i_{1},...,i_{p}},m_{i_{1},...,i_{p+1}})\quad\text{ and }\quad\hat{\xi}=(\hat{A}^{q-1}_{i},\hat{A}^{q-2}_{i_{1}i_{2}},...,\hat{A}^{0}_{i_{1},...,i_{q}},\hat{m}_{i_{1},...,i_{q+1}})

is defined in the usual way
[
Bry93
, §1.5]
[
Gom06
, Sec. 2.2]
by

\xi\cup\hat{\xi}:=(A^{p-1}_{i_{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{A}_{i_{1}}^{q-1}\;,\;...\;,\;A^{0}_{i_{1},...,i_{p}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{A}^{q-1}_{i_{p+1}}\;,\\
m_{i_{1},...,i_{p+1}}\hat{A}^{q-1}_{i_{p+1}}\;,\;...\;,\;m_{i_{1},...,i_{p+1}}\hat{A}^{0}_{i_{p+1},...,i_{p+q}}\;,\;m_{i_{1},...,i_{p+1}}\hat{m}_{i_{p+1},...,i_{p+q+1}})\text{.}

(6.3.2)

Of most importance for us is the cup product of two objects
\xi,\hat{\xi}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
. Namely, for
\xi=(A_{i},a_{ij},m_{ijk})
and
\hat{\xi}=(\hat{A}_{i},\hat{a}_{ij},\hat{m}_{ijk})
we obtain

\xi\cup\hat{\xi}=(A_{i}\wedge\hat{F}\;,\;a_{ij}\hat{F}\;,\;m_{ijk}\hat{A}_{k}\;,\;m_{ijk}\hat{a}_{kl}\;,\;m_{ijk}\hat{m}_{klp})\text{.}

(6.3.3)

Unfortunately, I have not been able to find a description for the cup product of morphisms in
\mathcal{H}^{p}(X)
. Kahle-Valentino just claim in
[
KV14
, §A.2]
that the cup product “extends” to morphism, but do not explain how, whereas the obvious attempt, namely to apply formula
eq.

6.3.2
to cochains, does not work. Concretely, we need the cup product of two morphisms
[\eta]:\xi\to\xi^{\prime}
and that
[\hat{\eta}]:\hat{\xi}\to\hat{\xi}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
, i.e.,
\xi^{\prime}=\xi+\mathrm{D}\eta
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}=\hat{\xi}+\mathrm{D}\hat{\eta}
. Suppose
\eta=(p_{i},z_{ij})
and
\hat{\eta}=(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{z}_{ij})
.
The only way that I was able to produce a 3-cochain
\eta\cup\hat{\eta}
such that
\xi^{\prime}\cup\hat{\xi}^{\prime}=\xi\cup\hat{\xi}+\mathrm{D}(\eta\cup\hat{\eta})
is

\eta\cup\hat{\eta}=(p_{i}\hat{F}\;,\;z_{ij}\hat{A}_{j}\;,\;z_{ij}\hat{a}_{jk}-m^{\prime}_{ijk}\hat{p}_{k}\;,\;m^{\prime}_{ijk}\hat{z}_{kl}+z_{ij}\hat{m}_{jkl})\text{.}

(6.3.4)

In the following, I assume that this is the correct cup product of morphisms in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
.

Exploring the notion of a differential T-duality pair further, we spell out in the following what a geometric trivialization of
\xi\cup\hat{\xi}
from
eq.

6.3.3
is. It consists of:

1.

a 3-form
K\in\Omega^{3}(X)

2.

2-forms
R_{i}\in\Omega^{2}(U_{i})
, such that

A_{i}\wedge\hat{F}=K+\mathrm{d}R_{i}\text{.}

(6.3.5)

3.

1-forms
\varphi_{ij}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
such that

a_{ij}\hat{F}=-R_{j}+R_{i}+\mathrm{d}\varphi_{ij}.

(6.3.6)

4.

functions
b_{ijk}:U_{i}\cap U_{j}\cap U_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

m_{ijk}\hat{A}_{k}=\varphi_{ij}+\varphi_{jk}-\varphi_{ik}+\mathrm{d}b_{ijk}.

(6.3.7)

5.

numbers
q_{ijkl}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

m_{ijk}\hat{a}_{kl}=q_{ijkl}+b_{ijk}+b_{ikl}-b_{ijl}-b_{jkl}

(6.3.8)

and

m_{ijk}\hat{m}_{klp}=q_{ijkl}-q_{ijkp}+q_{ijlp}-q_{iklp}+q_{jklp}\text{.}

(6.3.9)

At this point, it makes sense to discuss the action of
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)
on differential T-duality pairs, which is induced by the above-mentioned action of
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)
on all geometric trivializations of
\xi\cup\hat{\xi}
.
Here, this action takes the form

\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)\times\mathrm{TDP}(X)\to\mathrm{TDP}(X)

and is given, using the above description of geometric trivializations, by the formula

((B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk},s_{ijkl}),(K,R_{i},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl}))\mapsto(K+\mathrm{d}B_{i},R_{i}+B_{i},\varphi_{ij}+A_{ij},b_{ijk}+c_{ijk},q_{ijkl}+s_{ijkl})\text{.}

Note that
K
is shifted by the globally defined 3-form
H=\mathrm{d}B_{i}
, the curvature.
It is clear that this action restricts to the fibres of the map
\mathrm{TDP}(X)\to\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)
and is transitive in each fibre.

As in
section

6.2
, see
proposition

6.2.6
, we consider the pair
(F,\hat{F})\in\Omega^{2}(X)\times\Omega^{2}(X)
determined by an element of
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)
, and the subgroup
\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}\subseteq\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)
.

Lemma 6.3.4
.

The subgroup
\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
acts trivially, and the quotient
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)/\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
acts freely and transitively in the fibre over
(\xi,\hat{\xi})
.

We have to show that
(K,R_{i},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl})
and
(K,R_{i}+\hat{y}F+y\hat{F},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl})
define the same morphism. We consider the automorphism of
\xi=(A_{i},a_{ij},m_{ijk})
given by
(y,0)
, this works as
\mathrm{D}(y,0)=(0,0,0)
, and similarly, the automorphism of
\hat{\xi}
given by
(\hat{y},0)
. According to
eq.

6.3.4
, we have

(y,0)\cup(\hat{y},0)=(y\hat{F}\;,\;0\;,\;-m_{ijk}\hat{y},\;0)

We can change this by the coboundary of

(-\hat{y}A_{i},-\hat{y}a_{ij},0)
,
which is
(-\hat{y}F,0,\hat{y}m_{ijk},0)
.
Thus,

[(y,0)\cup(\hat{y},0)]=[(y\hat{F}-\hat{y}F,0,0,0)]\text{.}

This proves that
\mathcal{F}
acts trivially. Conversely, if

(K,R_{i},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl})\quad\text{ and }\quad(K+\mathrm{d}B_{i},R_{i}+B_{i},\varphi_{ij}+A_{ij},b_{ijk}+c_{ijk},q_{ijkl}+s_{ijkl})

are equivalent, we have to show that
(B_{i},A_{ij},c_{ijk},s_{ijkl})\sim(\hat{y}F-y\hat{F},0,0,0)
. The proof of this is very similar to the one given in
proposition

6.2.6
, and omitted for brevity.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to give the proof of
proposition

6.3.3
.
Under
propositions

5.5.1
and
6.2.4
, it remains to provide a bijection

\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(X,\mathbb{TD}_{\kappa})\to\mathrm{TDP}(X).

(6.3.10)

Let
(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i},\varphi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})
be an adjusted differential
\mathbb{TD}
-cocycle. We set
\xi=(A_{i},a_{ij},-m_{ijk})
and
\hat{\xi}=(\hat{A}_{i},\hat{a}_{ij},-\hat{m}_{ijk})
: this ensures that the diagram in
proposition

6.3.3
will commute, while the signs accounts for the different conventions used in non-abelian cohomology and Deligne cohomology. We define

K:=\mathrm{d}R_{i}+A_{i}\wedge\hat{F}\text{;}

(6.3.11)

this is the 3-curvature of
remark

6.2.2
, and hence a globally defined 3-form. Thus, passing to
-R_{i}
, we have
eq.

6.3.5
. We may then use the given 1-form
\varphi_{ij}
, and note that
eq.

6.2.3
results into
eq.

6.3.6
.
Next, we choose real-valued functions
b_{ijk}
that represent the given
\mathbb{T}
-valued functions
-t_{ijk}
; then,
eq.

6.2.1
results into
eq.

6.3.7
.
Finally, we consider
eq.

5.6.2
,

t_{ikl}+t_{ijk}-m_{ijk}\hat{a}_{kl}=t_{ijl}+t_{jkl}\text{,}

which is an equation of
\mathbb{T}
-valued functions.
Substituting the lifts
b_{ijk}
reveals
q_{ijkl}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

-b_{ikl}-b_{ijk}-m_{ijk}\hat{a}_{kl}=-b_{ijl}-b_{jkl}+q_{ijkl}\text{,}

this is
eq.

6.3.8
.
Finally,
eq.

6.3.9
is a straightforward calculation. Summarizing,

(K,R_{i},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl})
is a geometric trivialization of
\xi\cup\hat{\xi}
.

Next we consider an equivalence between adjusted differential cocycles

(A_{i},\hat{A}_{i},R_{i},\varphi_{ij},a_{ij},\hat{a}_{ij},m_{ijk},\hat{m}_{ijk},t_{ijk})\quad\text{ and }\quad(A_{i}^{\prime},\hat{A}_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime},\varphi_{ij}^{\prime},a_{ij}^{\prime},\hat{a}_{ij}^{\prime},m_{ijk}^{\prime},\hat{m}_{ijk}^{\prime},t_{ijk}^{\prime})

established by a tuple
(\phi_{i},p_{i},\hat{p}_{i},z_{ij},\hat{z}_{ij},\tilde{e}_{ij})
.
Then,
\eta:=(-p_{i},z_{ij})
and
\hat{\eta}:=(-\hat{p}_{i},\hat{z}_{ij})
are morphisms in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(X)
between
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}:=(A_{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{ij},-m^{\prime}_{ijk})
, and
\hat{\xi}
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}=(\hat{A}^{\prime}_{i},\hat{a}_{ij}^{\prime},-\hat{m}^{\prime}_{ijk})
, respectively. We have to show that

(K,R_{i},\varphi_{ij},b_{ijk},q_{ijkl})\sim(K^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime},\varphi_{ij}^{\prime},b_{ijk}^{\prime},q_{ijkl}^{\prime})+\eta\cup\hat{\eta}\text{.}

We claim that both cochains differ in fact by the coboundary of
(\phi_{i},z_{ij}\hat{p}_{j}+f_{ij},-r_{ijk}+z_{ij}\hat{z}_{jk})
, where
f_{ij}
is a real-valued lift of
\tilde{e}_{ij}
, and
r_{ijk}\in\mathbb{Z}
are the numbers that emerge from the
\mathbb{T}
-valued cocycle condition
eq.

5.6.3
under this lift. The claim is straightforward to check using
eq.

6.3.4
.

By now we have constructed a well-defined map
eq.

6.3.10
, such that it preserves the fibres of the projections to
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)\times\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(X)
. It is easy to see that our map
eq.

6.3.10
is equivariant w.r.t. to the actions of
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{3}(X)/\mathcal{F}_{F,\hat{F}}
in each fibre. Since these actions are free and transitive on both sides (
lemmas

6.3.4
and
6.2.6
), it follows that
eq.

6.3.10
is a bijection. This proves
proposition

6.3.3
.

7
Examples of geometric T-duality

We first consider in
section

7.1
the situation of a general principal
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-bundle
E
, a general metric, and trivial B-field, and present a construction of a T-dual geometric T-background. In
section

7.2
we specialize to the case that
E
is the Hopf fibration, in which we explicitly compute the dual metric and dual bundle gerbe. In
section

7.3
we keep the Hopf fibration but consider a non-trivial B-field, whose Dixmier-Douady class is a generator of
\mathrm{H}^{3}(S^{3},\mathbb{Z})
. We prove that this geometric T-background is self-dual.

7.1
A torus bundle with trivial B-field

We consider a geometric T-background
(E,g,\mathcal{G})
over a smooth manifold
X
, whose bundle gerbe is the trivial one, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{0}
. In this section, we explicitly construct a geometric T-duality correspondence whose left leg is
(E,g,\mathcal{I}_{0})
.

We let
(\omega,g^{\prime},h)
be the triple corresponding to
g
under
theorem

2.3.1
, and we let
F\in\Omega^{2}(X)
be the curvature of the connection
\omega
. We consider the trivial bundle
\hat{E}:=X\times\mathbb{T}^{n}
, and equip it with the trivial connection,
\hat{\omega}:=\theta
. We let
\hat{g}
be the invariant metric on
\hat{E}
that corresponds to the triple
(\hat{\omega},g^{\prime},h^{-1})
. Next we construct the bundle gerbe
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
over
\hat{E}
.

The surjective submersion is
Y:=E\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\to X\times\mathbb{T}^{n}
. The curving is

\Psi:={\mathrm{pr}}_{E}^{*}\omega\wedge{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}^{*}\theta\in\Omega^{2}(Y)\text{.}

The 2-fold fibre product is
Y^{[2]}=E^{[2]}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Note that we have a smooth map
g:E^{[2]}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
,
e_{2}=e_{1}g(e_{1},e_{2})
, and
{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\omega={\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\omega+g^{*}\theta
. Thus, we see that

{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\Psi-{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\Psi=g^{*}\theta\dot{\wedge}{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}^{*}\theta

on
Y^{[2]}
.
Comparing with
remark

2.2.1
, the right hand side is the curvature of the pullback of the Poincaré bundle
\mathcal{P}
along the map
\tilde{g}:E^{[2]}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{2n}:(e_{1},e_{2},a)\mapsto(a,g(e_{1},e_{2}))
.
Thus, we readily define

P:=\tilde{g}^{*}\mathcal{P}

as the principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundle with connection of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. Over
Y^{[3]}=E^{[3]}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}
, we have an isomorphism

({\mathrm{pr}}_{23}^{*}P\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{12}^{*}P)_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},a)}=\mathcal{P}_{a,g(e_{2},e_{3})}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{a,g(e_{1},e_{2})}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{r}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}_{a,g(e_{2},e_{3})g(e_{1},e_{2})}=\mathcal{P}_{a,g(e_{1},e_{3})}=({\mathrm{pr}}_{13}^{*}P)_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},a)}\text{,}

where
\varphi_{r}
was defined in
section

2.2
.
This isomorphism satisfies the associativity condition over
Y^{[4]}
due to the commutativity of the analog of
eq.

2.2.1
for
\varphi_{r}
.

Remark 7.1.1
.

If
n=1
, then
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is precisely the
cup product bundle gerbe

{\mathrm{pr}}_{X}^{*}E\cup{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}}
, where
{\mathrm{pr}}_{X}:\hat{E}\to X
and
{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}}:\hat{E}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
are the projections; explicitly,
{\mathrm{pr}}_{X}^{*}E
is a principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundle over
\hat{E}
with connection, and
{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a
\mathbb{T}
-valued function on
\hat{E}
. A description of the cup product of such structures, resulting in a bundle gerbe with connection, has been given by Johnson in
[
Joh02
]
. Our construction above (for
n=1
) reproduced exactly that description. Johnson also proved that the cup product of a principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundle with connection and a
\mathbb{T}
-valued function coincides with the cup product in Deligne cohomology
[
Joh02
]
.

We will now construct a geometric T-duality correspondence between the geometric T-backgrounds
(E,g,\mathcal{G})
and
(\hat{E},\hat{g},\hat{\mathcal{G}})
. On correspondence space
E\times_{X}\hat{E}
we need to find a connection-preserving isomorphism
\mathcal{D}:{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rho_{g,\hat{g}}}
, where

\rho_{g,\hat{g}}=\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\omega}\dot{\wedge}{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\omega=-\Psi\text{.}

We note that
\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is trivializable since its surjective submersion has a section
\sigma
along
\hat{\mathrm{pr}}
, namely, the identity,
\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{E\times\mathbb{T}^{n}}
:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 39.14766pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 63.14766pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E\times\mathbb{T}^{n}=Y\hskip-20.00003pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.7512pt\raise-23.80554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-39.14766pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E\times_{X}\hat{E}=E\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.36385pt\raise 0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.36385pt\raise-0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.36385pt\raise 0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.36385pt\raise-0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.36385pt\raise 0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.36385pt\raise-0.93146pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.82817pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.82817pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\times\mathbb{T}^{n}=\hat{E}\text{.}\hskip-20.00003pt}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

It induces a trivialization
\mathcal{S}:\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathcal{I}_{\Psi}
, and
\mathcal{D}
may be defined as

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 41.27094pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-41.27094pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{0}=\mathcal{I}_{\Psi}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-\Psi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.46533pt\raise 6.53668pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.70334pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.271pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 81.271pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rho_{g,\hat{g}}}\text{.}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Thus,
\mathcal{D}
is a geometric correspondence. It remains to check that it is a geometric T-duality correspondence.

Conditions
and
of
definition

4.1.9
hold by construction of the metric
\hat{g}
. In order to check condition
, we consider an open subset
U\subseteq X
that admits a trivialization
\varphi:U\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\to E|_{U}
. On the dual side, we choose the identity trivialization,
\hat{\varphi}=\mathrm{id}
. We put
B:=0
and
\mathcal{T}:=\mathrm{id}
, as a trivialization of
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\varphi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{I}_{B}
. Note that
\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\hat{\mathcal{G}}|_{U\times\mathbb{T}^{n}}
. Thus, the surjective submersion of
\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}
has a global section,
\tau:=(\varphi,{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}):U\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\to E\times\mathbb{T}^{n}
. It induces a trivialization
\hat{\mathcal{T}}:\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathcal{I}_{\tau^{*}\Psi}
. We put

\hat{B}:=\tau^{*}\Psi=\varphi^{*}\omega\dot{\wedge}{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}^{*}\theta\text{.}

Now we work over
U\times\mathbb{T}^{2n}
, where we find the diagram

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{E}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

whose rectangular part is commutative, but the sections differ. This means that the induced trivializations
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
and
\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{T}}
differ by the
\mathbb{T}
-bundle with connection

(\sigma\circ\Phi,\tau\circ\hat{\mathrm{pr}})^{*}P\text{;}

a discussion of this fact can be found in
[
Wal16
, Lem. 3.2.3]
.
We readily compute the map

k:=\tilde{g}\circ(\sigma\circ\Phi,\tau\circ\hat{\mathrm{pr}}):U\times\mathbb{T}^{2n}\to\mathbb{T}^{2n}:(x,a,\hat{a})\mapsto(\hat{a},\hat{a}-a)\text{.}

We note that
k^{*}\mathcal{P}\cong\mathcal{P}_{3,3-2}\cong\mathcal{P}_{3,3}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{3,-2}\cong\mathcal{P}_{2,3}
, using the results of
section

2.2
.
The 2-isomorphism
\hat{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\hat{\mathcal{T}}\cong\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\otimes k^{*}\mathcal{P}
implies that the relevant isomorphism of

,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern corresponds to the principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundle
k^{*}\mathcal{P}\cong{\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2n}}^{*}\mathcal{P}
. This completes the proof the we have a geometric T-duality correspondence.
In particular, by
proposition

4.2.1
, the Buscher rules hold locally.

7.2
The Hopf fibration with a trivial B-field

In this section we apply the construction of the previous
section

7.1
to the example where the torus bundle
E
is the Hopf fibration
E:=S^{3}\to S^{2}
. This reproduces a result from the PhD thesis of Kunath
[
Kun21
, §3.4, §4.4]
, where that case has been discussed separately.

We denote the round metric on the
n
-sphere by
g_{n}
; the metric on
E
is
g=g_{3}
, which is indeed
\mathbb{T}
-invariant. Then, the dual torus bundle is
\hat{E}:=S^{2}\times\mathbb{T}
. This was probably the first observation of a topology change, and made in
[
AAGBL94
]
. There, the following result has been proved, by applying locally the Buscher rules. Here, we re-derive it by applying the general procedure of
section

7.1
.

Lemma 7.2.1
.

The dual metric is
\hat{g}=\frac{1}{4}g_{2}\oplus g_{1}
.

We claim that
g^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4}g_{2}
and
h=g_{1}
. Then,
remark

2.3.3

applied to
\hat{E}
and the trivial connection
A=0
yields the lemma. The claim can be proved in an explicit model for the Hopf fibration. We model
p:S^{3}\to S^{2}
as the restriction to unit vectors of the map

\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}:(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\mapsto(2(x_{0}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{3}),2(x_{1}x_{2}-x_{0}x_{3}),x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}-x_{3}^{2})\text{.}

The action of
z\in\mathbb{T}
sends
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
to
(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
, where

x_{0}^{\prime}+\mathrm{i}x_{1}^{\prime}:=z\cdot(x_{0}+\mathrm{i}x_{1})\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{2}^{\prime}+\mathrm{i}x_{3}^{\prime}:=z\cdot(x_{2}+\mathrm{i}x_{3})\text{.}

The tangent space
T_{x}S^{3}
at
x\in S^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
is
x^{\perp}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
. The round metric
g_{3}
is given by the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, i.e.,
g_{3}(v,w):=v\cdot w
. (One can see now directly that it is
\mathbb{T}
-invariant.) The differential of the bundle projection
p
at
x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is

T_{x}p=2\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}&x_{3}&x_{0}&x_{1}\\
-x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}&-x_{0}\\
x_{0}&x_{1}&-x_{2}&-x_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}\text{.}

One computes

V_{x}=\mathrm{Kern}(T_{x}p)=\left\langle(-x_{1},x_{0},-x_{3},x_{2})\right\rangle

and thus,

h_{p(x)}(r,s):=g_{3}((-rx_{1},rx_{0},-rx_{3},rx_{2}),(-sx_{1},sx_{0},-sx_{3},sx_{2}))=rs\text{.}

In particular, this metric does not depend on the base point
p(x)
.
We observe that
T_{x}p\cdot T_{x}p^{\mathrm{tr}}=4E_{4}
, where
E_{4}
denotes the unit matrix. We know that
T_{x}p|_{H_{x}}:H_{x}\to T_{p(x)}X
is an isomorphism, and so
\frac{1}{4}T_{x}p^{\mathrm{tr}}
is a right inverse. Thus,

g(v,w):=\textstyle g_{3}(\frac{1}{4}T_{x}p^{\mathrm{tr}}(v),\frac{1}{4}T_{x}p^{\mathrm{tr}}(w))=\frac{1}{4}vw=\frac{1}{4}g_{2}(v,w)\text{.}

This proves the claim.
∎

By
remark

7.1.1
, the dual bundle gerbe
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is the cup product
\hat{\mathcal{G}}={\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{2}}^{*}E\cup{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{1}}
of the principal
\mathbb{T}
-bundle
{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{2}}^{*}E
and the
\mathbb{T}
-valued function
{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{1}}
. Summarizing, we have the following result.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

Let
E:=S^{3}\to S^{2}
be the Hopf fibration,
g:=g_{3}
be the round metric, and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{0}
be the trivial bundle gerbe. Then, there exists a geometric T-duality correspondence between
(E,g,\mathcal{G})
and the geometric T-background
(S^{2}\times S^{1},\frac{1}{4}g_{2}\oplus g_{1},\hat{\mathcal{G}})
, where
\hat{\mathcal{G}}={\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{2}}^{*}E\cup{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{1}}
is the cup product bundle gerbe. In particular, the Dixmier-Douady class of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is a cup product in singular cohomology,

\mathrm{DD}(\hat{\mathcal{G}})={\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{2}}^{*}c_{1}\cup{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{1}}^{*}\theta\text{,}

where
c_{1}
is the first Chern class of the Hopf fibration, a generator of
\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{2},\mathbb{Z})
, and
\theta\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{1},\mathbb{Z})
is a generator. Thus,
\mathrm{DD}(\hat{\mathcal{G}})
is a generator of
\mathrm{H}^{3}(S^{2}\times S^{1},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, the H-flux of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is

\hat{H}={\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{2}}^{*}F\wedge{\mathrm{pr}}_{S^{1}}^{*}\theta\text{,}

where
F\in\Omega^{2}(S^{2})
is the curvature of the Kaluza-Klein connection corresponding to the metric
g=g_{3}
.

As remarked above, the dual metric has been computed in
[
AAGBL94
]
.
The formula for the dual H-flux has been proved
in the setting of T-duality with H-flux by Bouwknegt-Evslin-Mathai
[
BEM04b
]
. Our unifying setting of geometric T-duality correspondences implies
both results.

7.3
The Hopf fibration with the basic gerbe

The Hopf fibration
E:=S^{3}\to S^{2}
carries a canonical non-trivial bundle gerbe with connection, namely, the
basic gerbe

\mathcal{G}_{bas}
over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, under the canonical diffeomorphism
S^{3}\cong{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
. In this section, we consider this bundle gerbe, while we keep
E
equipped with the round metric
g_{3}
as in
section

7.2
.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

The geometric T-background
(S^{3},g_{3},\mathcal{G}_{bas})
is self-dual under geometric T-duality.

In the setting of T-duality with H-flux, the self-duality of
(S^{3},g_{3},H)
, where
H\in\Omega^{3}(S^{3})
is the curvature of the basic gerbe, i.e., the canonical 3-form, was known before;
proposition

7.3.1
upgrades this to geometric T-duality.

In the remainder of this section we prove
proposition

7.3.1
. We recall that the diffeomorphism between
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
and
S^{3}
is

{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\to S^{3}:\begin{pmatrix}a&-\bar{b}\\
b&\bar{a}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mapsto(a,b)\text{;}

here, the resulting element
(a,b)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
is identified with
(\mathrm{Re}(a),\mathrm{Im}(a),\mathrm{Re}(b),\mathrm{Im}(b))\in S^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
. It is well-known that the round metric
g_{3}
on
S^{3}
corresponds to the Killing form
B(Y_{1},Y_{2})=4\mathrm{tr}(Y_{1}Y_{2})
on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
. More precisely, we have under above diffeomorphism

g_{3}=-\frac{1}{8}B\text{.}

Under the diffeomorphism with
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, the principal
\mathbb{T}
-action of the Hopf fibration is a map
\tau:{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times\mathbb{T}\to{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, and it is given by matrix multiplication along the group homomorphism

\zeta:\mathbb{T}\to{\mathrm{SU}}(2):z\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&\bar{z}\\
\end{pmatrix}

In other words, we have
\tau=m\circ\ (\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)
, where
m
denotes the multiplication map of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
.
We also remark that
\zeta(\mathbb{T})
is a maximal torus.
Now we consider the basic bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}_{bas}
over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)

[
GR02
,
Mei02
]
. Its canonical connection has the curvature
H=\frac{1}{6}\left\langle\theta\wedge[\theta\wedge\theta]\right\rangle
, where
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle
is the basic inner product, which, in case of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, is
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle=-\mathrm{tr}(-\cdot-)=-\frac{1}{4}B
. We recall that
\mathcal{G}_{bas}
also has a canonical multiplicative structure
[
CJM+05
,
Wal10
]
, consisting of a connection-preserving isomorphism

\mathcal{M}:{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{G}\to m^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rho}

over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, where
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\theta\wedge{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\bar{\theta}\right\rangle\in\Omega^{2}({\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2))
; here
\bar{\theta}
is the
right
-invariant Maurer-Cartan form. Additionally, there is an “associator”, a connection-preserving 2-isomorphism

\alpha:\mathcal{M}_{1+2,3}\circ(\mathcal{M}_{1,2}\otimes\mathrm{id})\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1,2+3}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2,3})

over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)^{3}
, which in turn satisfies a pentagon axiom over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)^{4}
.

We consider another multiplicative bundle gerbe, but over the Lie group
\mathbb{T}
. The underlying bundle gerbe with connection is the trivial one,
\mathcal{I}_{0}
. It is equipped with a multiplicative structure using the method of
[
Wal10
, Ex. 1.4 (b)]
. Its multiplication isomorphism

\mathcal{P}:{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{0}\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{0}\to m^{*}\mathcal{I}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\Omega}

over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is given by the Poincaré bundle
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, under the equivalence of
proposition

2.1.4
. Its associator is

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.49315pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.49315pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 183.67406pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{1,3}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2,3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 263.74463pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi_{r}\otimes\mathrm{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 297.8549pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 297.8549pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{1,2+3}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2,3}\text{,}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

and one can easily check that the pentagon condition over
\mathbb{T}^{4}
is satisfied. The bundle gerbe
\mathcal{I}_{0}
together with the multiplicative structure will be denoted by
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathcal{P}}
.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

We have
\zeta^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\cong\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathcal{P}}
as multiplicative bundle gerbes with connection.

One considers for multiplicative bundle gerbes with connection the pair
(H,\rho)
consisting of the curvature
H
of the bundle gerbe and the 2-form
\rho
of their multiplicative structure. One can check that
\zeta^{*}(H,\rho)=(0,\Omega)
.
By
[
Wal10
, Prop. 2.4]
the pair
(H,\rho)
characterizes the multiplicative bundle gerbe uniquely up to isomorphism provided that
\mathrm{H}^{4}(BG,\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free. This is the case when
G=\mathbb{T}
, as the cohomology of
\mathbb{T}
is a polynomial ring.
∎

In the following we choose an isomorphism
\mathcal{T}:\zeta^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\to\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathcal{P}}
of multiplicative bundle gerbes with connection (it is unique up to unique 2-isomorphism).
The multiplicative structure
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{G}_{bas}
then induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

\tau^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}=(\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)^{*}m^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}}}{{\cong}}(\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)^{*}({\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-\rho})\\
={\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\zeta^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-(\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)^{*}\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{T}}}{{\cong}}{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-(\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)^{*}\rho}\text{.}

over
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times\mathbb{T}
. Next we infer that
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times\mathbb{T}
is canonically diffeomorphic to the correspondence space for the self-dual situation: the diffeomorphism is

\Psi:{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\times\mathbb{T}\to S^{3}\times_{S^{2}}S^{3}:(X,z)\mapsto(X,X\zeta(z))\text{.}

Note that
{\mathrm{pr}}\circ\Psi={\mathrm{pr}}_{1}
and
\hat{\mathrm{pr}}\circ\Psi=\tau
. Thus, pulling back the isomorphism
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

along
\Psi^{-1}
, we obtain a candidate for the isomorphism
\mathcal{D}
.
We first verify that the 2-form is correct, i.e.

\Psi^{*}\rho_{g_{3},g_{3}}=(\mathrm{id}\times\zeta)^{*}\rho\text{.}

This can be checked explicitly using the given definitions.
By this, we have a geometric correspondence.

Conditions
and
of
definition

4.1.9
are obviously satisfied, since we have the same bundle and metric on both sides.
It remains to verify condition
. Consider an open set
U\subseteq S^{2}
with a local trivialization
\varphi:U\times S^{1}\to S^{3}|_{U}
. We denote by
s:U\to S^{3}\cong{\mathrm{SU}}(2):x\mapsto\varphi(x,1)
the corresponding section. For dimensional reasons, there exists a trivialization
\mathcal{S}:s^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\to\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}
, where
\lambda\in\Omega^{2}(S^{2})
. Note that
\varphi(x,z)=s(x)\zeta(z)
, or,
\varphi=m\circ(s\times\zeta)
. Hence, we may produce a trivialization

\mathcal{U}:\varphi^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}=(s\times\zeta)^{*}m^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\cong{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}s^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\zeta^{*}\mathcal{G}_{bas}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-(s\times\zeta)^{*}\rho}\cong\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\lambda-(s\times\zeta)^{*}\rho}

with
B:={\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\lambda-(s\times\zeta)^{*}\rho
.
We choose the same trivializations
\varphi
and
\mathcal{U}
on both sides.

We observe that there is a commutative diagram

where
\psi(x,z_{1},z_{2}):=(s(x)\zeta(z_{1}),z_{2}-z_{1})
, and
\Phi=(\varphi,\varphi)
.
Over
U\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
we then have to consider the isomorphism

(\mathcal{U}_{1,3}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\psi^{*}\Psi^{*}\mathcal{D}\,\circ\,\mathcal{U}^{-1}_{1,2}

Substituting the definitions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{D}
, it turns our that all occurrences of
\mathcal{M}
, and both occurrences of
\mathcal{S}
cancel. Remaining are the contributions of
\mathcal{T}
, which are
\mathcal{T}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2-1}
. By
lemma

7.3.2
, this gives the Poincaré bundle.
This proves
, and completes the proof of
proposition

7.3.1
.
